Algebra 1 — Unit 3 Bank

Systems and Constraints · Framework v6.34 · Bank profile v1.0

Section 3.1 Section 3.2 Section 3.3 Section 3.4 Section 3.5 Secure Summative By I-Can / Parallel View

Section 3.1: Graphing and Elimination

Learning target: Students will solve systems graphically and by elimination while interpreting the meaning of different solution types.

WTC

What’s to Come

U3-31-WTC-001 · WTC · Section 3.1

Two plans are shown on the same graph. Without using a formal system-solving algorithm, describe what the intersection tells you, estimate the intersection, and explain what would be different if the two lines never met.

Answer / solution

The intersection is about $(6,7)$ and represents the input where both plans have the same output. Parallel distinct lines would mean there is no shared solution.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can construct graphical representations of systems and connect intersection points to solutions.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Preview the section through one coherent shared situation/representation.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Example 3

U3-31-NOTES-EXAMPLE-3-001 · Notes Example 3 · Section 3.1

Solve by elimination the system. $\begin{cases}x+y=8\\2x-y=1\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

Adding the equations gives $3x=9$, so $x=3$ and $y=5$. The solution is $(3,5)$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use elimination to solve systems and interpret the solution type.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

You Try It 3

U3-31-NOTES-YTI-3-001 · Notes YTI 3 · Section 3.1

Solve by elimination the system.

$\begin{cases}2x - y = 9\\ x + 2y = 2\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

Solution: $(4,-1)$. Substitution into both original equations verifies the ordered pair.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use elimination to solve systems and interpret the solution type.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Apply the just-modeled idea independently.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Example 4

U3-31-NOTES-EXAMPLE-4-001 · Notes Example 4 · Section 3.1

A student tries to eliminate $y$ from $2x+y=7$ and $3x-y=8$ by subtracting the equations. Identify the error and state the efficient elimination operation.

Answer / solution

The $y$-coefficients are opposites, so the equations should be added, not subtracted. Adding gives $5x=15$, so $x=3$ and $y=1$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use elimination to solve systems and interpret the solution type.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

You Try It 4

U3-31-NOTES-YTI-4-001 · Notes YTI 4 · Section 3.1

To eliminate $x$ from $2x+3y=12$ and $x-y=1$, a student multiplies only the $x$-term in the second equation by $-2$. Identify and correct the error.

Answer / solution

A multiplier must apply to every term on both sides. The correct transformed equation is $-2x+2y=-2$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use elimination to solve systems and interpret the solution type.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Apply the just-modeled idea independently.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Example 5

U3-31-NOTES-EXAMPLE-5-001 · Notes Example 5 · Section 3.1

Classify the system as one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions. $\begin{cases}y=2x+1\\ y=2x-4\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

No solution. The lines have the same slope and different intercepts, so they are parallel.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can determine whether a system has one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions from algebraic and graphical evidence.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

You Try It 5

U3-31-NOTES-YTI-5-001 · Notes YTI 5 · Section 3.1

Classify the system as one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions. $\begin{cases}y=-x+3\\2x+2y=6\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

Infinitely many solutions. The second equation simplifies to $x+y=3$, which is the same line as $y=-x+3$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can determine whether a system has one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions from algebraic and graphical evidence.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Apply the just-modeled idea independently.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Example 6

U3-31-NOTES-EXAMPLE-6-001 · Notes Example 6 · Section 3.1

Classify the system as one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions. $\begin{cases}y=\frac12x-2\\ y=-x+4\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

One solution. The lines have different slopes, so they intersect exactly once.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can determine whether a system has one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions from algebraic and graphical evidence.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

You Try It 6

U3-31-NOTES-YTI-6-001 · Notes YTI 6 · Section 3.1

Classify the system as one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions. $\begin{cases}3x-y=5\\6x-2y=14\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

No solution. Doubling the first equation gives $6x-2y=10$, not $14$, so the equations represent distinct parallel lines.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can determine whether a system has one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions from algebraic and graphical evidence.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Apply the just-modeled idea independently.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Practice Set 1

Question 1

U3-31-PRACTICE-SET-1-001 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.1

A student multiplies only one term of $x+2y=7$ by $-2$ before elimination. Identify the mistake and write the correct equivalent equation.

Answer / solution

The multiplier must apply to every term on both sides. The correct equation is $-2x-4y=-14$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use elimination to solve systems and interpret the solution type.

Question 4

U3-31-PRACTICE-SET-1-004 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.1

Solve by elimination. $\begin{cases}2x+3y=10\\-2x+y=6\end{cases}$

Answer / solution

The solution is $(-1,4)$. Adding suitable equivalent equations eliminates one variable; substitution into both originals verifies the ordered pair.

The multiplier must apply to every term and to the right side. The correct equivalent equation is $-3x-9y=9$.

Question 3

U3-31-EXIT-TICKET-A-003 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.1

Use elimination to solve $\begin{cases}2x+y=7\\-2x+3y=5\end{cases}$. State the ordered-pair solution and classify the system as having one solution, no solution, or infinitely many solutions.

Answer / solution

Adding the equations gives $4y=12$, so $y=3$. Then $2x+3=7$, so $x=2$. The solution is $(2,3)$, so the system has one solution.

Section 3.2: Solving Systems by Substitution

Learning target: Students will solve systems using substitution and choose efficient forms for solving.

WTC

What’s to Come

U3-32-WTC-001 · WTC · Section 3.2

A system contains $y=2x-1$ and $3x+y=14$. Explain what could replace $y$ in the second equation, predict why that replacement preserves the same solution, and estimate the ordered pair that should satisfy both equations.

Answer / solution

Replace $y$ with $2x-1$ because they are equal. Then $3x+(2x-1)=14$, giving $x=3$ and $y=5$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can formulate a substitution plan when one equation is already written in an efficient form.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Preview the section through one coherent shared situation/representation.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Notes

Example 1

U3-32-NOTES-EXAMPLE-1-001 · Notes Example 1 · Section 3.2

For the system $y=3x-5$ and $2x+y=11$, identify the most efficient substitution step before solving.

Answer / solution

Substitute $3x-5$ for $y$ in the second equation because $y$ is already isolated.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can formulate a substitution plan when one equation is already written in an efficient form.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

You Try It 2

U3-32-NOTES-YTI-2-001 · Notes YTI 2 · Section 3.2

Which method is more efficient for $x+2y=11$ and $3x-2y=5$: substitution or elimination? Explain the structural cue.

Answer / solution

Elimination is more efficient because the $y$-coefficients are opposites, so adding the equations eliminates $y$ immediately.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can assess which algebraic method is more efficient for a given system.

Answer / solution

The entire expression must replace $y$: $4x-2(2x-3)=10$.

Which method is more efficient for $3x+y=7$ and $y=x-1$: substitution or elimination? Explain the structural cue.

Answer / solution

Substitution is more efficient because one equation is already solved for a variable.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can assess which algebraic method is more efficient for a given system.

Question 9

U3-32-PRACTICE-SET-1-009 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.2

A student substitutes $-2x-3$ for $y$ in $5x-3y=15$ but writes $5x-3(-2x)-3=15$. Identify and correct the substitution error.

Answer / solution

The entire expression must replace $y$: $5x-3(-2x-3)=15$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply substitution to solve systems and interpret the ordered-pair solution.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.

Question 10

Check whether $(3,-2)$ satisfies both $y=x-5$ and $x+y=2$.

Answer / solution

No. The first equation is true because $-2=-2$, but the second gives $1\ne2$.

Question 2

Which method is more efficient for $x+y=0$ and $y=-2x-1$: substitution or elimination? Explain the structural cue.

Answer / solution

Substitution is more efficient because one equation is already solved for $y$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can assess which algebraic method is more efficient for a given system.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Independent Intro-stage evidence on the current Unit 3 section I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 3

U3-32-EXIT-TICKET-A-003 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.2

Use substitution to solve $\begin{cases}y=2x-3\\ x+y=9\end{cases}$. State the ordered-pair solution and explain what the ordered pair means for the two equations.

Answer / solution

Substitute $2x-3$ for $y$: $x+(2x-3)=9$, so $x=4$ and $y=5$. The ordered pair $(4,5)$ satisfies both equations.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply substitution to solve systems and interpret the ordered-pair solution.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Independent Intro-stage evidence on the current Unit 3 section I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 4

U3-32-EXIT-TICKET-A-004 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.2

Check whether $(1,-4)$ satisfies both $y=-3x-1$ and $x+y=-2$.

Answer / solution

No. The first equation is true because $-4=-4$, but the second gives $-3\ne-2$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply substitution to solve systems and interpret the ordered-pair solution.
Representation: Equation
Evidence/job: Independent Intro-stage evidence on the current Unit 3 section I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Section 3.3: Writing and Solving Systems from Situations

Learning target: Students will create and solve systems that model real-world situations.

WTC

What’s to Come

U3-33-WTC-001 · WTC · Section 3.3

A school event sold 40 tickets. Student tickets cost 5 dollars and adult tickets cost 8 dollars; total revenue was 248 dollars. Define two variables, identify the two independent constraints, predict what each coordinate of a solution would mean, and describe a reasonable way to check the result.

Answer / solution

Let $s$ and $a$ be student and adult tickets. The constraints are $s+a=40$ and $5s+8a=248$. Solving gives $s=24$, $a=16$; both equations and the nonnegative whole-number context provide checks.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can build a system of equations that represents two constraints in a situation.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Preview the section through one coherent shared situation/representation.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Notes

Example 1

U3-33-NOTES-EXAMPLE-1-001 · Notes Example 1 · Section 3.3

Using $x$ for adult tickets and $y$ for student tickets, solve $x+y=25$ and $8x+5y=170$. Interpret both coordinates in context.

Answer / solution

$x=15$, $y=10$. These values are the numbers of adult tickets and student tickets, respectively.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

xlarge boxes costsmall boxes count needed to total 20
0020
53515
107010
Answer / solution

Question 4

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-1-004 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.3

A group sells premium passes for 12 dollars each and regular passes for 7 dollars each. It sold 20 items for 180 dollars total. A candidate solution is $(8,12)$. What checks confirm that this solution is reasonable in context?

Answer / solution

Check both constraints: $8+12=20$ and $12(8)+7(12)=180$. The values are also nonnegative whole numbers, so they fit an item-count context.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.

Question 2

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-002 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

The table lists combinations of large candles and small candles that meet one total-item constraint. Describe the relationship and write the count equation.

x = large candlesy = small candles
034
826
1717
Answer / solution

Each increase of 1 in $x$ requires a decrease of 1 in $y$ to keep the total at 34; $x+y=34$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can build a system of equations that represents two constraints in a situation.
Representation: Table
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 3

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-003 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

A group sells large candles for 8 dollars each and small candles for 5 dollars each. It sold 9 items for 54 dollars total. A candidate solution is $(3,6)$. What checks confirm that this solution is reasonable in context?

Answer / solution

Check both constraints: $3+6=9$ and $8(3)+5(6)=54$. The values are also nonnegative whole numbers, so they fit an item-count context.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 4

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-004 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

A group sells premium passes for 12 dollars each and regular passes for 7 dollars each. They sell 7 items for 64 dollars. Define variables and write a system that models the situation.

Answer / solution

Let $x$ be the number of premium passes and $y$ the number of regular passes. A valid system is $x+y=7$ and $12x+7y=64$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can build a system of equations that represents two constraints in a situation.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 5

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-005 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

Two cost plans are graphed. Estimate the break-even point and explain what it means in the situation.

Answer / solution

The lines meet at $(4,7)$. At an input of 4 units, the two plans have the same cost/output of 7.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can predict the meaning of a solution before solving from the context and variables.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Use a graph to interpret the solution of two contextual constraints.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 6

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-006 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

Using $x$ for team shirts and $y$ for caps, solve $x+y=18$ and $14x+6y=164$. Interpret both coordinates in context.

Answer / solution

$x=7$ and $y=11$. These values are the numbers of team shirts and caps, respectively.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 7

U3-33-PRACTICE-SET-2-007 · Practice Set 2 · Section 3.3

Before solving $x+y=10$ and $14x+6y=100$ for team shirts and caps, explain what each coordinate of the ordered-pair solution will represent.

Answer / solution

The first coordinate is the number of team shirts; the second is the number of caps. Both coordinates must satisfy the count and value constraints.

Question 1

U3-33-EXTRA-PRACTICE-001 · Extra Practice · Section 3.3

A model for counts of notebooks and pens produces the candidate $(11,16)$. Explain what must be checked before accepting it as a reasonable contextual solution.

Answer / solution

Verify the candidate in both original constraints and confirm both coordinates are nonnegative whole numbers because they count notebooks and pens.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.

Question 2

U3-33-EXTRA-PRACTICE-002 · Extra Practice · Section 3.3

A fundraiser sells notebooks for 5 dollars each and pens for 2 dollars each. A total of 25 items brings in 80 dollars. Define variables and write a system.

Answer / solution

Let $x$ be notebooks and $y$ be pens. Then $x+y=25$ and $5x+2y=80$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can build a system of equations that represents two constraints in a situation.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.

Check Your Understanding 6

U3-33-CYU-006 · CYU · Section 3.3

A group sells notebooks for 5 dollars each and pens for 2 dollars each. It sold 22 items for 80 dollars total. A candidate solution is $(12,10)$. What checks confirm that this solution is reasonable in context?

Answer / solution

Check both constraints: $12+10=22$ and $5(12)+2(10)=80$. The values are also nonnegative whole numbers, so they fit an item-count context.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can design a solution pathway that connects equations, calculations, and a contextual conclusion.

U3-33-WARM-UP-1-001 · Warm-Up 1 · Section 3.3

Complete the Diamond. The top is the product and the bottom is the sum.

Answer / solution

The side numbers are $3$ and $6$ because $3\cdot6=18$ and $3+6=9$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply operation rules to calculate sums, differences, products, and quotients with rational numbers.
Representation: Equation

Question 2

U3-33-WARM-UP-1-002 · Warm-Up 1 · Section 3.3

A water tank begins with 12 liters and fills at 5 liters per minute. Write a linear model for the amount $A$ after $t$ minutes and interpret the rate.

Answer / solution

$A=5t+12$. The rate means the tank gains 5 liters each minute.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: Use linear models to represent and interpret real-world data.

Evidence/job: Prior/review retrieval connected to the section.

Question 1

U3-33-EXIT-TICKET-C-001 · Exit Ticket C · Section 3.3

A club pays a fixed fee of 5 dollars plus 3 dollars per participant. The total is 26 dollars. Write and solve an equation for the number of participants.

Answer / solution

$5+3p=26$, so $p=7$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: Write, solve, and interpret equations that model situations.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Independent Mastery/portfolio evidence; mapped only where the stage skill supports the named Mastery Goal.
Mastery Goal: U3-MG04
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 4

U3-33-EXIT-TICKET-C-004 · Exit Ticket C · Section 3.3

A service charges a 7-dollar fixed fee plus 4 dollars per hour. The total charge is 31 dollars. Write and solve an equation for the number of hours.

Answer / solution

$7+4h=31$, so $h=6$ hours.

Section 3.4: Graphing Linear Inequalities

Learning target: Students will graph and interpret linear inequalities.

WTC

What’s to Come

U3-34-WTC-001 · WTC · Section 3.4

The graph shows a shaded half-plane. Describe what the dashed boundary communicates, choose a point from the shaded region and explain how it could be checked, and predict how the graph would change if the boundary were included.

Answer / solution

The dashed line means boundary points are excluded. A shaded point should make the inequality true when substituted. If the boundary were included, the line would be solid.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can critique a graph of a linear inequality using the boundary, shading, and solution region.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Preview the section through one coherent shared situation/representation.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Notes

Example 1

U3-34-NOTES-EXAMPLE-1-001 · Notes Example 1 · Section 3.4

Write an inequality represented by the shaded graph. Include the correct inequality symbol.

Answer / solution

One correct inequality is $y \le 2x+1$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can critique a graph of a linear inequality using the boundary, shading, and solution region.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Test pointLeft side yBoundary valueSatisfies?
(0,0)04No
Answer / solution

You Try It 2

U3-34-NOTES-YTI-2-001 · Notes YTI 2 · Section 3.4

Use the test-point table to decide whether $(0,0)$ belongs in the solution region for $y\le 2x+1$.

Test pointLeft side $y$Boundary valueSatisfies?
$(0,0)$01Yes
Answer / solution

Yes. At $(0,0)$, $0\le 1$ is true, so the point belongs in the solution region.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use test points to interpret which region satisfies an inequality.

The shading is on the correct side, but the boundary must be dashed because the inequality is strict.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can critique a graph of a linear inequality using the boundary, shading, and solution region.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Complete the Diamond. The top is the product and the bottom is the sum.

Answer / solution

The side numbers are $4$ and $-5$ because $4\cdot(-5)=-20$ and $4-5=-1$.

Question 4

U3-34-EXIT-TICKET-A-004 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.4

For $y\lt-2x+3$, use the test point $(0,0)$ to decide which side of the boundary should be shaded. Show the substitution and state whether the half-plane containing $(0,0)$ is part of the solution.

Answer / solution

Substitute $(0,0)$: $0\lt-2(0)+3$, so $0\lt3$ is true. Therefore the side containing $(0,0)$ is shaded; this is the region below the dashed boundary $y=-2x+3$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can use test points to interpret which region satisfies an inequality.
Representation: Test Point + Equation
Evidence/job: Independent Intro-stage evidence on the current Unit 3 section I-can.

Section 3.5: Linear Systems of Inequalities

Learning target: Students will analyze and interpret systems of linear inequalities.

WTC

What’s to Come

U3-35-WTC-001 · WTC · Section 3.5

Two constraints are graphed at once. Describe why the darker overlap matters, choose one point that is feasible for both constraints, and explain why a point in only one shaded region is not a solution of the system.

Answer / solution

The overlap contains points satisfying both inequalities. For example, $(2,4)$ is feasible. A point in only one region violates the other constraint.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can extend graphing strategies to represent overlapping solution regions.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Preview the section through one coherent shared situation/representation.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Notes

Example 1

U3-35-NOTES-EXAMPLE-1-001 · Notes Example 1 · Section 3.5

Use the graph to describe the feasible region of the system. Give one point that appears feasible.

Answer / solution

The feasible region is the overlap of both shaded half-planes. For example, $(1,4)$ lies in the overlap.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can extend graphing strategies to represent overlapping solution regions.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Model a canonical method/representation tied to the exact I-can.

Example 3

U3-35-NOTES-EXAMPLE-3-001 · Notes Example 3 · Section 3.5

A workshop can build at most 18 stools and tables total, and must build at least 6 tables. Let $s$ be stools and $t$ be tables. Write a system of inequalities.

Answer / solution

$s+t\le18$, $t\ge6$, with $s\ge0$ and $t\ge0$ if nonnegativity constraints are included.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can formulate a system of inequalities that represents multiple constraints.

U3-35-NOTES-EXAMPLE-4-001 · Notes Example 4 · Section 3.5

Explain how the word “system” changes the requirement from satisfying one inequality to satisfying both.

Answer / solution

A feasible point must satisfy every inequality in the system, so it must lie in the overlap of all solution regions.

Answer / solution

No. A feasible point must satisfy every inequality in the system, so a point outside either solution region is outside their overlap.

Question 10

U3-35-PRACTICE-SET-1-010 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.5

The point $(1,10)$ satisfies $y\ge -x+5$ but does not satisfy $y\le x+8$. Can it belong to the feasible region of the system? Explain using the meaning of overlap.

Answer / solution

No. A feasible point must satisfy every inequality in the system, so a point outside either solution region is outside their overlap.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply feasible-region reasoning to interpret possible solutions in context.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 11

U3-35-PRACTICE-SET-1-011 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.5

Use the graph to describe the feasible region of the system. Give one point that appears feasible.

Answer / solution

The feasible region is the overlap of both shaded half-planes. For example, $(-1,2)$ lies in the overlap.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can extend graphing strategies to represent overlapping solution regions.
Representation: Graph
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Question 12

U3-35-PRACTICE-SET-1-012 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.5

Determine whether $(2,6)$ is feasible for $y\le 2x+5$ and $y\ge 0.5x+2$. Show both checks.

Answer / solution

$6\le 9$ is true and $6\ge 3$ is true; the point is feasible.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply feasible-region reasoning to interpret possible solutions in context.
Representation: Equation

Question 15

U3-35-PRACTICE-SET-1-015 · Practice Set 1 · Section 3.5

A workshop can make at most 20 tables in total and must make at least 3 of type $y$. Let $x$ and $y$ be the two types. Write a system of inequalities, including nonnegativity.

Answer / solution

$x+y\le 20$, $y\ge 3$, $x\ge0$, and $y\ge0$.

What is the difference between a solution to one inequality and a feasible solution to a system of inequalities?

Answer / solution

A solution to one inequality meets one constraint; a feasible system solution meets all constraints simultaneously.

A workshop can make at most 26 basic packages and premium packages in total and must make at least 7 premium packages. Let $x$ be basic packages and $y$ be premium packages. Write a system of inequalities, including nonnegativity.

Answer / solution

$x+y\le 26$, $y\ge 7$, $x\ge0$, and $y\ge0$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can formulate a system of inequalities that represents multiple constraints.
Representation: Context

Question 14

U3-35-PRACTICE-SET-3-014 · Practice Set 3 · Section 3.5

Why is a point that satisfies only one inequality not a solution of the whole system?

Answer / solution

A system requires every inequality to be true at the same point; satisfying only one constraint is not enough.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply feasible-region reasoning to interpret possible solutions in context.
Representation: Context
Evidence/job: Create learning through purposeful fluency, representation use, or diagnostic reasoning.
Source basis: Unit 3 Assessment Plan; Framework section enrichment

Use the graph to describe the feasible region of the system. Give one point that appears feasible.

Answer / solution

Why should a contextual feasible point also be checked against quantity restrictions such as nonnegativity?

Answer / solution

Graph overlap handles the written inequalities, while context restrictions determine whether the coordinates make sense for the modeled quantities.

Check Your Understanding 6

U3-35-CYU-006 · CYU · Section 3.5

The point $(3,11)$ satisfies $y\ge -x+5$ but does not satisfy $y\le x+7$. Can it belong to the feasible region of the system? Explain using the meaning of overlap.

Answer / solution

No. A feasible point must satisfy every inequality in the system, so a point outside either solution region is outside their overlap.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply feasible-region reasoning to interpret possible solutions in context.

Complete the Diamond. The top is the product and the bottom is the sum.

Answer / solution

The side numbers are $4$ and $6$ because $4\cdot6=24$ and $4+6=10$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can apply operation rules to calculate sums, differences, products, and quotients with rational numbers.

Question 4

U3-35-WARM-UP-2-004 · Warm-Up 2 · Section 3.5

A rule assigns each input exactly one output. Explain why this condition matters when deciding whether a relationship is a function.

Answer / solution

A function must give one output for each input; an input paired with two different outputs violates the function rule.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: Interpret functions as rules that connect inputs and outputs without relying on formal domain and range language.

Question 3

U3-35-WARM-UP-3-003 · Warm-Up 3 · Section 3.5

A relation pairs input $3$ with output $5$ and also pairs the same input $3$ with output $8$. Explain whether the relation can be a function and why.

Answer / solution

It is not a function because the single input $3$ is paired with two different outputs, $5$ and $8$.

A relation contains $(1,4)$, $(2,6)$, $(1,7)$, and $(3,8)$. Is it a function? Explain using the input-output rule.

Answer / solution

Question 3

U3-35-EXIT-TICKET-A-003 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.5

A group has a limit of 19 total items split between museum tickets and student tickets, and it needs at least 9 student tickets. Let $x$ be the number of museum tickets and $y$ the number of student tickets. Write a system of inequalities, including nonnegativity.

Answer / solution

$x+y\le19$, $y\ge9$, $x\ge0$, and $y\ge0$.

Alignment / evidenceExact target: I can formulate a system of inequalities that represents multiple constraints.

Question 4

U3-35-EXIT-TICKET-A-004 · Exit Ticket A · Section 3.5

The point $(2,8)$ satisfies $y\ge -x+4$ but does not satisfy $y\le x+5$. Can it belong to the feasible region of the system? Explain using the meaning of overlap.

Answer / solution

No. A feasible point must satisfy every inequality in the system, so a point outside either solution region is outside their overlap.

Question 3

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V5-003 · Secure Summative V5 · Section Secure

A student rewrites $4(2x-3)+5$ as $8x-12+5=8x-17$. Identify the error and give the correct equivalent expression.

Answer / solution

The arithmetic $-12+5$ equals $-7$, not $-17$. The correct expression is $8x-7$.

Question 4

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V5-004 · Secure Summative V5 · Section Secure

A taxi trip has a 6-dollar starting fee and costs 3 dollars per mile. A rider paid 27 dollars. Write and solve an equation for the miles, then explain why your result is reasonable.

Answer / solution

$6+3m=27$, so $m=7$. Seven miles gives $6+21=27$ dollars and is nonnegative.

Question 5

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V5-005 · Secure Summative V5 · Section Secure

The equation $y=2x+1$ is paired with the table $(0,1),(1,3),(2,6)$. Determine whether the representations agree and use specific evidence to support your answer.

Answer / solution

They do not agree. The equation gives $y=5$ when $x=2$, but the table gives 6.

Table A has outputs 2, 5, 8, 11. Table B has outputs 3, 6, 12, 24 for consecutive integer inputs. Classify each family and state the evidence you used.

Answer / solution

A is linear because of constant difference 3. B is exponential because of constant ratio 2.

Question 8

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V5-008 · Secure Summative V5 · Section Secure

A school club spends 48 dollars on a fixed room fee and 7 dollars per participant. Write an equation for a total cost of 104 dollars, solve it, and interpret the result.

Answer / solution

$48+7p=104$, so $p=8$. Eight participants produce the stated total.

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V6-001 · Secure Summative V6 · Section Secure

A machine adds 5 to twice every input. Write its function rule, generate outputs for inputs 0, 2, and 5, and explain why it is a function.

Answer / solution

$f(x)=2x+5$; outputs are 5, 9, and 15. Each input has exactly one output.

Question 2

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V6-002 · Secure Summative V6 · Section Secure

Two students claim $3(x+4)-2$ and $3x+10$ are equivalent. Verify or refute the claim using both structure and one substitution value.

Answer / solution

They are equivalent: distribution gives $3x+12-2=3x+10$. For example, at $x=2$ both equal 16.

A table contains $(0,-1),(2,3),(4,7)$. Determine the linear equation and describe where the graph would cross the $y$-axis. Explain how the table supports both features.

Answer / solution

The slope is 2 and the intercept is -1, so $y=2x-1$ and the graph crosses at $(0,-1)$.

A streaming plan charges 9 dollars each month plus a one-time 15-dollar setup fee. A customer has paid 69 dollars total. Determine the number of months and verify the result in the original relationship.

Answer / solution

$15+9m=69$, so $m=6$. Checking gives $15+54=69$.

Without fully expanding both expressions first, decide whether $5(x-2)+3x$ and $8x-10$ are equivalent. Explain which structural step makes the comparison clear.

They are equivalent. Distributing only the first factor gives $5x-10+3x=8x-10$.

A tank contains 12 liters and fills at 4 liters per minute. Write an equation and a table for minutes 0, 1, 2, and 3. Explain how the initial value and rate appear in both.

Answer / solution

$A=4t+12$; table outputs 12,16,20,24. The 12 is the starting amount and the constant increase 4 is the rate.

Question 7

U3-SUM-SECURE-SUMMATIVE-V6-007 · Secure Summative V6 · Section Secure

A student solves $4n+10=34$ and reports $n=11$. Diagnose the error, solve correctly, and interpret $n$ if it represents the number of 4-dollar items after a 10-dollar fixed charge.

Answer / solution

$4n=24$, so $n=6$. The student likely subtracted incorrectly or divided the wrong quantity. Six items cost 24 dollars plus the 10-dollar charge.
